That re-render then: (a) recomputes `const records = useMemo(() => getPersonalRecords(workoutLog), [workoutLog])` (line 1131) — a full O(sessions×exercises×sets) scan; (b) re-renders `history.map(session => <HistoryCard key={session.id} session={session} unit={unit} />)` (line 1294) with `HistoryCard` declared as a plain `React.FC` (line 893, no `React.memo`), and each card calls `sessionSetCount(session)` (line 908), `sessionVolume(session)` inline in JSX (line 960) and `formatDate(session.date)` (line 916/934) which goes through `toLocaleDateString` (src/core/utils/calculations.ts:140).

**User impact.** For a user 6 months in (~78 sessions, ~1,500 sets), every character typed into a weight or reps box walks ~1,500 sets three times and re-renders 78 collapsed history cards including 78 Intl date formats. The set grid stops keeping up with the keyboard, so logging "3 × 8 @ 100" between sets — the thing the screen exists for — stutters.

**Fix.** Budget: set-log tap / keystroke to visual feedback < 100ms regardless of log size. Split the screen so the live session does not share a subscription with history: select `activeWorkoutId` and the active session by id (`useStore(s => s.workoutLog.find(w => w.id === s.activeWorkoutId))` is still a new ref, so instead keep `workoutLog` only in the history section), move history + records into a child component with its own selector, wrap `HistoryCard` in `React.memo`, and hoist `sessionSetCount`/`sessionVolume`/`formatDate` into a `useMemo` inside the memoized card.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Verified. WorkoutScreen subscribes the whole array at workout.tsx:1116 `const workoutLog = useStore(s => s.workoutLog)`. immer's updateSet (appState.ts:537-544) mutates the set, so produce replaces set -> sets -> exercise -> exercises -> session -> workoutLog, giving a new array identity per character. That re-render re-runs `records = useMemo(() => getPersonalRecords(workoutLog), [workoutLog])` (1131) — a full O(sessions x exercises x sets) scan (workoutMath.ts:121-181) — and re-renders every `HistoryCard` at 1294-1296. HistoryCard is a plain React.FC (893) with no React.memo (grep for React.memo across app/ and src/ returns zero hits, confirmed), and calls sessionSetCount(908), sessionVolume inline at 960 and formatDate at 916/934 which goes through toLocaleDateString (calculations.ts:137-141). Everything in the finding checks out. Only the severity is wrong: the set grid gets slow, not unusable, and no data is lost — P1.

</details>

<details><summary><b>PR detection and progression suggestions re-run over the whole log on every keystroke</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:675</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `const priorRecords = useMemo(() => { ... getPersonalRecords(workoutLog.filter(entry => entry.id !== session.id)) ... }, [workoutLog, session.id])` (675-681) — `workoutLog` changes on every `updateSet`, so both the `.filter` allocation and the full `getPersonalRecords` scan re-run per character. `const prSetIds = useMemo(..., [exercises, priorRecords])` (684-700) and `const suggestions = useMemo(() => { ... suggestNextSet(workoutLog, exercise.liftId, exercise.lift) ... }, [exercises, workoutLog])` (702-710) have the same deps; `suggestNextSet` internally calls `getLiftHistory` which does a full `filter` + `sort` of the log per unique lift (src/core/utils/workoutMath.ts:98-105, 192).

**User impact.** With 6 exercises in a session and 78 sessions logged, one character typed into a reps box triggers ~8 full traversals of the entire workout log (one `getPersonalRecords`, six `getLiftHistory` sort+filter passes). Combined with the screen-level re-render above, the set grid is the slowest input in the app precisely when the user is between sets and wants to move fast.

**Fix.** Budget: set-grid keystroke work < 5ms of JS. `priorRecords` only needs to change when a session is finished, not while sets are being edited — key it off `workoutLog.length` plus `activeWorkoutId` instead of the array identity, or compute it once when the session starts and hold it in a ref. Same for `suggestions`: the suggestion is a function of *closed* sessions only, so derive it from `workoutLog.filter(isCompletedSession)` memoized on `workoutLog.length`.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** All three memos verified at the cited lines. workout.tsx:675-681 `priorRecords` deps are [workoutLog, session.id] and it does `workoutLog.filter(...)` plus a full getPersonalRecords scan; 684-700 prSetIds depends on [exercises, priorRecords]; 702-710 `suggestions` depends on [exercises, workoutLog] and calls suggestNextSet per unique liftId, which calls getLiftHistory -> full filter + slice + sort of the log (workoutMath.ts:98-105) then filters to completed sessions (192). Since workoutLog identity changes on every updateSet, all of this re-runs per character, on top of the screen-level cascade. The map.has guard at 705 does correctly dedupe per liftId, so 'six getLiftHistory passes for six distinct lifts' is accurate.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The persisted blob has no partialize and grows without bound (including base64 photos)</b> — <code>src/store/useStore.ts:25</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `persist(immer(createAppState), { name: 'macrofit-storage', storage: ..., onRehydrateStorage: ... })` passes no `partialize`, so the default identity partialize persists every key of AppState — `diary` (every day ever logged), `workoutLog` (every session), `weightLog`, `mealTemplates`, `customFoods`, and `progressPhotos`, whose type is `{ id, date, dataUrl: string /* base64 compressed JPEG */, pose, notes? }` (src/core/types/index.ts:186-192) capped at 20 entries by `addProgressPhoto` (src/core/store/appState.ts:425-428). Nothing in this app writes photos, but `hydrateStore` explicitly pulls `'progressPhotos'` down from the cloud (appState.ts:609, mirrored in src/lib/AuthProvider.tsx:43), so a user who used the web photo feature inherits them. Also persisted: transient `activeWorkoutId`, `fastingSession`.

**User impact.** A FoodEntry serializes to ~500 bytes (an 18-field Food object inlined per entry). At 8 entries/day for 6 months that is ~1,440 entries ≈ 0.7 MB, plus ~78 sessions of workout data ≈ 0.2 MB — so ~1 MB of JSON, and up to ~4 MB more if progress photos synced down. That whole blob is re-stringified on every mutation and re-parsed on every cold start, adding hundreds of ms before the splash can be dismissed and growing forever.

**Fix.** Budget: persisted store < 500 KB after 12 months of daily logging; cold-start rehydrate < 150ms. Add `partialize: s => ({ profile, currentWeightKg, goals, diary, weightLog, mealTemplates, customFoods, recentFoodIds, streak, darkMode, bodyMeasurements, onboardedAt, recommendation, recommendationSeenAt, workoutLog, customLifts, workoutTemplates })` — drop `progressPhotos` (they belong in the filesystem via expo-file-system, referenced by URI) and drop `activeWorkoutId`/`fastingSession` if a stale active session is acceptable. Add a diary/workout retention window (e.g. keep 400 days locally, rely on the cloud row for older data).

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. src/store/useStore.ts:25-31 passes only name/storage/onRehydrateStorage, so zustand's default identity partialize (middleware.js:475-477) persists every key of AppState — diary, workoutLog, weightLog, mealTemplates, customFoods, progressPhotos, plus transient activeWorkoutId (appState.ts:206) and fastingSession (199). ProgressPhoto really does carry a base64 JPEG in `dataUrl` (src/core/types/index.ts:185-191), addProgressPhoto caps at 20 (appState.ts:425-428), and hydrateStore explicitly pulls 'progressPhotos' down from the cloud (appState.ts:609, mirrored at AuthProvider.tsx:43) even though nothing on mobile writes them. Everything re-stringifies on every mutation and re-parses on cold start. See also my 'missed' entry on the 6 MB AsyncStorage cap, which turns this growth into silent write failure rather than just slowness.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The splash is released before auth and cloud hydration, leaving a blank canvas for up to 8s</b> — <code>app/_layout.tsx:100</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `const ready = (fontsLoaded || !!fontError) && hydrated` (100), `if (!ready) return null` (110) — `AuthProvider` is only mounted *after* `ready` (113-119), so `supabase.auth.getSession()` and `loadUserData` do not start until fonts and AsyncStorage are done. `SplashScreen.hideAsync()` fires as soon as `ready` (103, 107). The first route is app/index.tsx, which does `if (loading) return null` (22), and `loading` stays true until `loadUserData` resolves — which races an explicit `new Promise<null>(resolve => setTimeout(() => resolve(null), 8000))` (src/lib/AuthProvider.tsx:63-66).

**User impact.** On a cold start with a slow or flaky connection the user sees the branded splash disappear and then an empty stone-coloured screen with no spinner, no logo and no text for up to 8 seconds before the dashboard appears. It reads as a crash or a hang, not as loading.

**Fix.** Budget: cold start to interactive dashboard < 2s; never more than 250ms of blank screen. Start the Supabase session restore in parallel with the font/store gate (move `AuthProvider` above the `if (!ready) return null`, or kick off `getSession()` at module scope), and fold auth into the splash gate: `const ready = fontsReady && hydrated && !authLoading`. If cloud hydration must be slow, render the app against the locally-rehydrated store and show the sync state rather than returning `null` from app/index.tsx.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified line by line. app/_layout.tsx:100 `ready = (fontsLoaded || !!fontError) && hydrated`; SplashScreen.hideAsync() fires on that alone at 103 and 107; `if (!ready) return null` at 110 means AuthProvider is not even mounted until 115, so supabase.auth.getSession() (AuthProvider.tsx:110) and loadUserData do not start until after the splash is already gone. app/index.tsx:22 then returns null while `loading` is true, and loading only clears when loadUserData settles, racing the explicit 8 s timeout at AuthProvider.tsx:63-66. Nothing renders in that window except the Stack's contentStyle backgroundColor (_layout.tsx:66). The comment at app/index.tsx:15-16 claims 'the native splash is still up at that point' — it is not, which is exactly the bug.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Confirming a Health Connect import commits one store write per weigh-in, each re-serializing the whole store</b> — <code>src/components/StepsCard.tsx:25</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `useHealthSync()` is called by `StepsCard` (line 25, mounted from the Dashboard at app/(tabs)/index.tsx:169) and again by `ProfileScreen` (app/(tabs)/profile.tsx:213). The hook has no shared cache — each instance runs its own mount effect: `getAvailability()`, then `hasPermissions()`, then `refreshSteps()` which does `Promise.all([readTodaySteps(), readSteps(7)])` (src/hooks/useHealthSync.ts:54-73). Separately, app/(tabs)/progress.tsx:661 calls `getHistoricalData({ daysBack: 365 })` and then `handleConfirmImport` (672-689) loops `for (const w of healthSyncResult.weights) addWeightEntry(...)` — one store write, and therefore one full-store serialize + AsyncStorage write, per imported weigh-in.

**User impact.** Both tabs independently hit the Health Connect bridge on mount, doubling the native round-trips during the busiest moment of startup. Worse, confirming a Google Fit import of a year of weigh-ins (up to 365 entries) fires up to 365 sequential store commits, each one stringifying the whole store — the app freezes for seconds and may be killed as ANR.

**Fix.** Primary fix: add a bulk `addWeightEntries(entries)` action that merges the whole import inside a single set() — raise as an upstream web-repo change to src/core/store/appState.ts — and call it from progress.tsx:675 and useHealthSync.ts:95. Secondary (P3): hoist useHealthSync's probe into a module-level singleton so StepsCard and ProfileScreen share one Health Connect round-trip; drop the 'startup' framing.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Two separate claims, one wrong and one under-rated. WRONG: 'probed twice on startup'. useHealthSync is indeed called by StepsCard (line 25, mounted from index.tsx:169) and ProfileScreen (profile.tsx:213), and the hook has no shared cache (its mount effect at useHealthSync.ts:62-73 runs getAvailability -> hasPermissions -> refreshSteps per instance) — but expo-router bottom tabs are lazy, so the Profile instance only mounts when the user first opens Profile, which is not startup. UNDER-RATED: progress.tsx:672-689 handleConfirmImport loops `for (const w of healthSyncResult.weights) addWeightEntry(...)` after getHistoricalData({daysBack: 365}) at 661. Each addWeightEntry (appState.ts:319-330) is its own set(), which under the persist wrapper means a full JSON.stringify + AsyncStorage.setItem, AND re-sorts the whole weightLog (322) — so an N-entry import is N full-store serializations plus N sorts on the JS thread with no yield. useHealthSync.ts:87-100 importWeightHistory has the identical loop. That is a genuine multi-second freeze / ANR risk on a real import, which is a P1.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The unbounded persisted blob can exceed AsyncStorage's 6 MB Android cap, after which every local save silently fails</b> — <code>src/store/useStore.ts:27</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `storage: createJSONStorage(() => AsyncStorage)` persists the whole state (no partialize, line 25). On Android, react-native-async-storage is SQLite-backed with a hard per-database ceiling: node_modules/@react-native-async-storage/async-storage/android/config.gradle `getDatabaseSize()` returns 6 MB unless `AsyncStorage_db_size_in_MB` is set, and it is applied as `mMaximumDatabaseSize = BuildConfig.AsyncStorage_db_size * 1024L * 1024L` (ReactDatabaseSupplier.java:44). I grepped android/gradle.properties (63 lines) and it does not set that property, so this app builds with the 6 MB default. Meanwhile the persisted blob includes `progressPhotos`, up to 20 base64 JPEGs (src/core/types/index.ts:185-191, appState.ts:425-428) pulled down from the cloud by hydrateStore (appState.ts:609). When the write exceeds the cap, AsyncStorage.setItem rejects — and zustand fires it as `void setItem()` (node_modules/zustand/middleware.js:506), so the rejection is discarded and nothing in the app ever learns the save failed.

**User impact.** A user who used the web progress-photo feature syncs those photos down, the local blob crosses 6 MB, and from that point every local write silently stops persisting. The app looks fine until the next cold start, when it rehydrates the last blob that fit and everything logged since is gone. Silent data loss with no error surface.

**Fix.** Add `partialize` excluding `progressPhotos` (and `activeWorkoutId`/`fastingSession`) in src/store/useStore.ts:25, which is the same fix as persist-no-partialize and caps the realistic size at well under 1 MB. Independently, raise the Android ceiling by adding `AsyncStorage_db_size_in_MB=20` to android/gradle.properties, and wrap the storage object handed to createJSONStorage so setItem catches and surfaces a write failure instead of it being swallowed by `void`.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** found by verifier, not original auditor

</details>

#### P2 (11)

<details><summary><b>Full workout history renders inside the Screen ScrollView with no virtualization</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:1291</code> · performance · effort L</summary>

**Code.** `{history.length > 0 && (<View style={{ gap: spacing.sm }}><SectionTitle>History</SectionTitle>{history.map(session => <HistoryCard ... />)}</View>)}` renders inside `<Screen>`, which is a plain `ScrollView` (src/components/Layout.tsx:100-109) with no `removeClippedSubviews`. `history` is `workoutLog` minus the active session (line 1127-1130) and is never sliced or paginated. Same shape at line 1234 `visibleRecords.map(...)` once "Show all records" is pressed (line 1285).

**User impact.** ScrollView mounts every child eagerly. At 3 sessions/week for 6 months that is 78 HistoryCards ≈ 800+ native views created before the first frame of the Workout tab; at 2 years (~400 sessions) it is 4,000+ views and the tab takes seconds to appear or runs out of memory on a low-end Android. The user taps Workout to start training and stares at a white screen.

**Fix.** Budget: Workout tab focus → first frame < 300ms with 500 logged sessions. Convert the screen body to a `FlatList` (or FlashList) whose `data` is `history`, with the active session, empty state, templates and records rendered via `ListHeaderComponent` — `app/lift-picker.tsx:458` and `app/food-search.tsx:678` already show that pattern working with `Screen scroll={false}`. Give it `keyExtractor={s => s.id}` and `initialNumToRender={8}`.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Verified: workout.tsx:1291-1298 maps the whole `history` array (built at 1127-1130 with no slice/pagination) as direct children of `<Screen>`, and Screen is a plain ScrollView (Layout.tsx:99-109) with no removeClippedSubviews and no virtualization. visibleRecords.map at 1234 is the same shape once 'Show all records' (1285) is pressed. So the mount cost is real and unbounded. Severity is overstated though: the tab is lazily mounted once per app session, and the auditor's own 6-month figure (78 collapsed cards, ~800 views) is a few hundred ms of one-time mount, not a white screen for seconds. That is noticeable friction (P2); the multi-second case only appears at 2+ years of logging.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Set rows and exercise cards get fresh handler and style objects every render</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:586</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `sets.map((set, position) => <SetRow ... suggestion={placeholderFor(position)} isPR={prSetIds.has(set.id)} onChange={patch => onChangeSet(set.id, patch)} onRemove={() => onRemoveSet(set.id)} />)` (586-598) — new arrows per render, and `SetRow` (line 171) / `ExerciseCard` (line 447) / `HistoryCard` (line 893) are all plain `React.FC` with no `React.memo` anywhere in the repo (grep for `React.memo` across app/ and src/ returns zero hits). `exercises.map(exercise => <ExerciseCard ... onChangeSet={(setId, patch) => {...}} onRemoveSet={...} onAddSet={...} onRemoveExercise={...} />)` (789-816) is the same. Inside SetRow, `style={[numberInputStyle(theme, settled), { flex: 1 }]}` (298) and `{ width: COL_REPS }` (316) build brand-new style objects on every render, and `placeholderFor` (468-476) re-scans previous sets for every row.

**User impact.** Even if the store cascade were fixed, one edited set still forces every other set row in the session (20-30 rows in a full-body day) through a full render + native prop diff, plus a fresh TextInput style object each time. The checkmark on 'set completed' feels laggy rather than instant.

**Fix.** Budget: completing a set → checkbox fills < 100ms. Wrap `SetRow`, `ExerciseCard` and `HistoryCard` in `React.memo`; hoist the per-row handlers into `useCallback`s keyed by id inside `ExerciseCard` (or pass `set.id` down and have SetRow call `onChange(set.id, patch)`); memoize `numberInputStyle` per theme+flat combination at module scope with `StyleSheet.create` (two static variants — `flat` and not — are all that is needed).

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Facts all check out: workout.tsx:586-598 creates `onChange={patch => onChangeSet(set.id, patch)}` and `onRemove={() => onRemoveSet(set.id)}` fresh per render; 789-816 does the same for ExerciseCard; SetRow (171), ExerciseCard (447) and HistoryCard (893) are plain React.FC and grep confirms React.memo appears nowhere in app/ or src/; numberInputStyle(theme, settled) at 298/316/392 allocates a new TextStyle object per render; placeholderFor (468-476) re-scans preceding sets per row. But severity is inflated: with the store cascade fixed, re-rendering 20-30 lightweight rows on one edit is a few ms of JS, not a laggy checkmark — and the checkmark path (handleComplete, 231-236) already goes through a store write, so this is not the dominant cost there. P2.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Once Progress has been visited it stays mounted and re-runs its PR/volume scans on every unrelated store write</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/progress.tsx:624</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** ProgressScreen holds `const diary = useStore(s => s.diary)`, `const weightLog = useStore(s => s.weightLog)`, `const workoutLog = useStore(s => s.workoutLog)` (624-627). React Navigation bottom-tabs keeps a screen mounted after its first focus (no `unmountOnBlur` is set in app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx:192), so ProgressScreen keeps re-rendering while the user is on the Workout tab: `sessionsInRange` (752-755), `totalVolumeKg = sessionsInRange.reduce((t, s) => t + sessionVolume(s), 0)` and `totalSets` (756-757, both outside any useMemo), `volumeRows` → `weeklyVolumeByMuscle(workoutLog, ...)` (759-768) and `recentPRs` → `getPersonalRecords(workoutLog)` (770-776) all re-evaluate because `workoutLog` changed. The dashboard does the same for `diary` via `useStore(s => s.diary[today])` (index.tsx:92) and diary.tsx:673.

**User impact.** Typing into a workout set field pays the cost of the Progress tab's PR scan and volume-by-muscle aggregation as well, on a screen the user cannot even see. It roughly doubles the per-keystroke JS work once the user has visited Progress once in the session.

**Fix.** Gate only progress.tsx: useIsFocused() from @react-navigation/native around the workoutLog-derived memos (759-776), or key them off a cheap invalidation value. Drop the index.tsx/diary.tsx part of the recommendation — those selectors are already reference-stable and correct.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** The Progress half is correct: progress.tsx:624-627 subscribes diary/weightLog/workoutLog wholesale, app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx:192 sets no unmountOnBlur, so once Progress has been focused it stays mounted and its workoutLog-keyed derivations re-run on every unrelated write — sessionsInRange (752-755), the un-memoized reduces at 756-757, volumeRows (759-768) and recentPRs -> getPersonalRecords (770-776). The supporting claims about the other two screens are FALSE: app/(tabs)/index.tsx:92 selects `s.diary[today]` and diary.tsx:673 selects `s.diary[date]` — immer does not touch those object references when workoutLog changes, so the selector returns the identical object and useSyncExternalStore bails out; neither screen re-renders. index.tsx:97-104 even documents this deliberately. So the cost is one unfocused screen, not 'roughly doubles' across the app.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Every 1.5s pause uploads the entire store to Supabase</b> — <code>src/lib/AuthProvider.tsx:137</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `useStore.subscribe(() => { ... saveTimerRef.current = setTimeout(flushSave, SAVE_DEBOUNCE_MS) })` with `SAVE_DEBOUNCE_MS = 1500` (48). `flushSave` → `collect()` which does `for (const key of SYNC_FIELDS) out[key] = state[key]` over 20 top-level keys (93-98) → `supabase.from('user_data').upsert({ user_id, data: storeData })` (83-85). There is no diffing and no minimum interval between uploads; the debounce only coalesces writes closer than 1.5s apart.

**User impact.** During a workout the user types, pauses to lift, types again — each pause longer than 1.5s ships the whole ~1 MB payload (diary + full workout log + weight log) over the cellular radio. A 45-minute session can produce dozens of full-store uploads, burning battery and data, and each upload also re-serializes the same blob the AsyncStorage layer just serialized.

**Fix.** Budget: ≤ 1 cloud write per 30s of continuous editing; payload only grows with new data, not with history. Add a minimum interval (leading + trailing throttle at 15-30s) on top of the debounce, keep the existing background flush at line 151, and skip the upload when `JSON.stringify(collect())` is unchanged from the last successful save (cache the hash, not the string).

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Mechanism verified: AuthProvider.tsx:137-144 subscribes to every store change and schedules flushSave on a pure trailing 1500 ms debounce (48); flushSave (100-107) calls collect() (93-98) which copies all 20 SYNC_FIELDS, then upserts the whole blob (83-85). No diffing, no minimum interval, no payload delta. Severity though: this costs battery and cellular data, it does not break or confuse a core task — P2 under the stated rubric. (Worth noting the fix should also cover the syncStatus re-render churn this causes; see my 'missed' entry.)

</details>

<details><summary><b>Five simultaneous BlurViews composite on the dashboard, with dimezisBlurView on Android</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/index.tsx:222</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** On the Dashboard, mounted at once: the `Screen` header blur (src/components/Layout.tsx:69 `<BlurView tint={...} intensity={theme.glass.intensity + 20} experimentalBlurMethod={Platform.OS === 'android' ? 'dimezisBlurView' : undefined} style={StyleSheet.absoluteFill} />`), the hero `GlassSurface` (index.tsx:222) and the coach `GlassSurface` (index.tsx:370) each rendering their own BlurView via src/components/Glass.tsx:53, plus the tab-bar blur (app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx:61) and the always-mounted AssistantButton FAB blur (app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx:176) — five. Each `Screen` additionally mounts a `<Backdrop />` of three `LinearGradient`s (src/components/Layout.tsx:57, Backdrop.tsx:30-72). Profile is four blurs (profile.tsx:309 + header + tab bar + FAB); Workout is four (workout.tsx:719 + header + tab bar + FAB).

**User impact.** `dimezisBlurView` re-captures and re-blurs the view hierarchy behind each BlurView every frame. Scrolling the dashboard on Android means five live blur passes per frame over a screen that also holds an SVG MacroRing and three gradient blooms — the primary scroll of the app drops frames on mid-range devices, which reads as the app being cheap.

**Fix.** Keep all five surfaces glass (they are all explicitly permitted). Reduce cost within the rules instead: lower theme.glass.intensity on Android, gate `experimentalBlurMethod: 'dimezisBlurView'` behind a device-class check so low-tier Android falls back to the flat scrim the Glass.tsx:29-32 comment already says the overlay is designed to survive, and make sure the Backdrop gradients under each blur are static (they are). Measure with the Perf Monitor before changing anything structural.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** The count is correct — I verified five simultaneously mounted BlurViews on the Dashboard: Layout.tsx:69-74 (header, intensity+20, dimezisBlurView on Android), index.tsx:222 hero GlassSurface and index.tsx:370 coach GlassSurface both rendering Glass.tsx:53-59, plus _layout.tsx:61-66 (tab bar) and _layout.tsx:176-181 (always-mounted FAB, intensity+30). Backdrop.tsx also mounts three LinearGradients per Screen (Layout.tsx:57, Backdrop.tsx:30/45/60). The proposed FIX is the problem: the binding design rules in docs/MOBILE-DESIGN.md explicitly sanction glass for the tab bar, the FAB, headers content scrolls under, the dashboard hero card AND the coach plan card — every one of the five. Recommending the FAB and coach card be de-glassed asks for a design-rule violation, so it cannot stand as written. Severity is also P2: dropped frames while scrolling is noticeable friction, not a broken task.

</details>

<details><summary><b>USDA search fetches are never aborted and never cached</b> — <code>app/food-search.tsx:275</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** The effect sets `let cancelled = false` and returns `() => { cancelled = true }` (296-298) — that only suppresses the `setState`, it does not abort the request. `searchUSDA` (src/core/utils/usdaApi.ts:56) calls `fetch(...)` with no `signal` and no timeout, then `await res.json()` and maps every row. There is no result cache keyed by query, so backspacing from "chicken" to "chicke" issues a fresh network round-trip for a query that was already answered.

**User impact.** Typing "chicken breast" produces several overlapping in-flight HTTPS requests to api.nal.usda.gov; each abandoned one still downloads, parses and maps a 12-item JSON payload on the JS thread before being thrown away. On a slow connection the search list stalls while the device is busy with responses nobody will see, and DEMO_KEY's 30 req/min budget is burned three times faster than needed.

**Fix.** Budget: at most one in-flight USDA request at a time; a repeated query returns from cache in < 16ms. Thread an `AbortController` through `searchUSDA(query, limit, signal)` and call `controller.abort()` in the effect cleanup (src/core/utils/usdaApi.ts is generated — raise as an upstream web-repo change adding the `signal` parameter and a timeout). Add a small `Map<string, Food[]>` LRU in app/food-search.tsx keyed by the debounced query.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. app/food-search.tsx:275-299: the cleanup only flips `cancelled` (296-298) and there is a separate requestRef sequence guard (273, 288) — neither aborts the request. src/core/utils/usdaApi.ts:56 calls `fetch(...)` with no signal and no timeout, then awaits res.json() and maps every row (60-91). No query->results cache exists anywhere in the file. Correctly flagged as an upstream (src/core) change for the signal parameter. Note the 350 ms debounce at 266-269 means requests fire per settled query rather than per character, so the overlap is bounded — but abandoned responses are still downloaded, parsed and mapped, and DEMO_KEY's 30 req/min budget is still burned on them.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Food search rebuilds renderItem on every keystroke and its rows are not memoized</b> — <code>app/food-search.tsx:468</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `const renderRow = ({ item }: { item: Row }) => { ... }` is declared in the component body (468) and passed as `renderItem={renderRow}` (681), so its identity changes on every render — and `query` state updates on every character (263 `onChange={setQuery}` → `setQuery`), independently of the 350ms `debounced` value. `FoodRow` (197) is a plain `React.FC` with `onPress={() => openServingStep(item.food)}` created inline (478). No `getItemLayout`, `initialNumToRender`, or `windowSize` is set on the FlatList (678-703).

**User impact.** Each character typed into the search box re-renders every visible food row — and each row is a `Surface` (shadow + border) containing three `Pill`s and four `Text` nodes. The keyboard outruns the list on lower-end hardware, so the search box feels sticky exactly when the user is trying to log breakfast quickly.

**Fix.** Budget: search keystroke → glyph < 50ms. Wrap `FoodRow` in `React.memo`, hoist `renderRow` out with `useCallback` (its only dependency is `openServingStep`, already a `useCallback` at 370), and pass `item.food` plus a stable `onPress={openServingStep}` so the row calls `onPress(food)` itself. Add `initialNumToRender={8}` and `windowSize={5}`.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. `renderRow` is declared in the component body at food-search.tsx:468 and passed as renderItem at 681, so its identity changes on every render; VirtualizedList re-renders its cells when renderItem changes. FoodRow (197) is a plain React.FC with `onPress={() => openServingStep(item.food)}` allocated inline at 478. The FlatList at 677-703 sets no getItemLayout, initialNumToRender or windowSize. One citation error: `onChange={setQuery}` is at line 463 (`<SearchBar value={query} onChange={setQuery} />`), not 263 — line 263 is the quantity useState. The mechanism (query state updates per character independently of the 350 ms debounced value at 266-269) is right.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Collapsed Profile sections still build all their rows on every render</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/profile.tsx:546</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `Section` renders `{open && (<View ...>{children}</View>)}` (117-130), but `children` is a prop — the JSX is evaluated by the caller before `Section` decides whether to show it. So `weightLog.slice(0, 20).map(entry => ...)` (546-565), `bodyMeasurements.slice(0, 5).map(...)` (620-642), `customFoods.map(food => ...)` (655-675, unbounded) and `mealTemplates.map(template => ...)` (690-711, unbounded) all execute on every ProfileScreen render even while their sections are collapsed — including `formatDate` (Intl) per weight entry and per measurement. ProfileScreen also holds 16 separate `useStore` subscriptions (196-211), so any of `profile`, `weightLog`, `bodyMeasurements`, `customFoods`, `mealTemplates`, `streak` or `darkMode` changing re-runs all of it.

**User impact.** Typing a single character into the Height field re-renders Profile and rebuilds ~45 hidden row element trees plus ~25 Intl date formats that are never shown. On a long-lived account with many custom foods the Profile form inputs feel sluggish.

**Fix.** Budget: Profile field keystroke < 50ms; hidden content costs 0. Change `Section` to accept `children: () => React.ReactNode` and call it only when `open`, or split each section's body into its own component that reads its slice of the store itself so the parent never touches it while collapsed.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. Section renders `{open && (<View ...>{children}</View>)}` at profile.tsx:117-130, and `children` is a prop, so the caller has already evaluated the JSX. weightLog.slice(0,20).map (546-565), bodyMeasurements.slice(0,5).map (620-642), the unbounded customFoods.map (655-675) and mealTemplates.map (690-711) all run on every ProfileScreen render regardless of `open`, including formatDate (Intl) at 554, 559, 627 and 636 — up to 50 Intl calls per render. ProfileScreen holds 16 useStore subscriptions at 196-211 and local field state (name/age/heightCm at 227-229), so typing in any profile field re-renders all of it. The unbounded custom-foods/templates lists make this scale badly on a long-lived account.

</details>

<details><summary><b>60 icons are used but the whole ~1,750-icon lucide barrel is pulled into the bundle</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:12</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** Every screen imports from the package root: `import { Activity, AlertTriangle, Check, ChevronDown, Dumbbell, Gauge, Plus, Repeat, Sparkles, Timer, Trash2, Trophy, X } from 'lucide-react-native'` (16 files do this). That resolves via the package's `react-native` export condition to `dist/esm/lucide-react-native.mjs` (234 KB), which statically re-exports `dist/esm/icons/index.mjs` (104 KB) — and that file imports all ~1,750 icon modules (3,514 files, 12 MB, in dist/esm/icons). Metro does not tree-shake by default (metro.config.js is a plain `getDefaultConfig` + `withNativeWind`, no tree-shaking flag). The app actually uses 60 distinct icons.

**User impact.** Every icon in the library is compiled into the Hermes bytecode bundle the user downloads and the engine must load, for the sake of 60 of them. It inflates the APK/OTA download and adds module-graph work at startup, pushing cold start further past budget.

**Fix.** Budget: JS bundle contribution from icons < 40 KB; cold start to interactive < 2s. The package exposes a per-icon subpath (`"./icons/*": { "react-native": "./dist/esm/icons/*.mjs" }`), so switch to `import Check from 'lucide-react-native/icons/check'` (kebab-case file names). A tiny `src/components/icons.ts` re-exporting only the 60 used icons via subpaths keeps call sites unchanged.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified in node_modules. package.json exports maps '.' -> dist/esm/lucide-react-native.mjs (234,699 bytes) under the react-native condition, and dist/esm/icons/index.mjs (104,465 bytes) is a flat list of `export { default as X } from './x.mjs'` for 1,757 icon modules in dist/esm/icons. Under Babel's CJS transform those re-exports become eager top-level requires, so every icon module is bundled AND executed at startup. 15 app/src files import from the package root (workout.tsx:12-26 is representative). metro.config.js is a plain getDefaultConfig + withNativeWind with no tree-shaking, confirmed. The per-icon subpath ('./icons/*' -> dist/esm/icons/*.mjs) exists, so the proposed fix works as written.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The auth context value is a new object on every render and syncStatus cycles three times per save, re-rendering every consumer</b> — <code>src/lib/AuthProvider.tsx:207</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `<AuthContext.Provider value={{ user, session, loading, signIn, signUp, resendConfirmation, signOut, syncStatus, hydrating }}>` (207-209) builds a fresh object literal on every AuthProvider render, and signIn/signUp/resendConfirmation/signOut are re-created function expressions too, so there is no memoization at all. saveUserData drives syncStatus through three state transitions per save: `setSyncStatus('saving')` (81), `setSyncStatus(error ? 'error' : 'saved')` (86), then `setTimeout(() => setSyncStatus('idle'), 2000)` (87). Because the auto-save fires on any 1.5 s pause in editing (137-144), a workout session produces one of these cycles every couple of seconds. Every useAuth consumer re-renders three times per cycle: RootNavigator (app/_layout.tsx:29, which re-renders the whole Stack), app/index.tsx:19 and ProfileScreen (app/(tabs)/profile.tsx:194).

**User impact.** While logging sets or editing profile fields, the whole navigation tree and the Profile screen re-render several times a minute for a status string almost nobody is looking at. On Profile this compounds with the eager collapsed sections (profile.tsx:546-711), which rebuild ~45 hidden rows plus ~50 Intl date formats each time.

**Fix.** Wrap the context value in useMemo keyed on [user, session, loading, syncStatus, hydrating] and wrap signIn/signUp/resendConfirmation/signOut in useCallback. Better still, move syncStatus into its own small context (or a zustand slice) so the high-churn value does not sit in the same provider as the low-churn auth identity.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** found by verifier, not original auditor

</details>

<details><summary><b>Every Text, Surface and Button subscribes to the store through useTheme, so one keystroke runs a thousand selectors</b> — <code>src/theme/useTheme.ts:13</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** useTheme is implemented as `const darkMode = useStore(s => s.darkMode)` (useTheme.ts:12-14), i.e. every call site is a live zustand subscription. It is called by Body/Label/StatValue (src/components/Text.tsx:9, 96, 119), Button and IconButton (src/components/Button.tsx:44, 123), Surface/GlassSurface (src/components/Glass.tsx:42, 86), Pill and Field (src/components/Layout.tsx:122, 158) and ProgressTrack (MacroRing.tsx:92). A single collapsed HistoryCard is a Surface plus ~10 Text nodes plus its own three selectors (workout.tsx:897-899), so roughly 14 subscriptions each; with the unvirtualized history list at workout.tsx:1294 rendering 78 cards that is ~1,100 live subscriptions on the Workout tab alone. zustand runs every subscriber's selector and equality check on every set(), so each character typed into a weight box pays that fan-out before any component even decides to re-render.

**User impact.** A fixed per-keystroke tax that grows linearly with how much the user has logged — it is the reason the set grid degrades with history size even for edits that touch nothing on screen. It also means virtualizing the history list (workout-history-unvirtualized) pays off twice: fewer mounted views AND far fewer store subscribers.

**Fix.** Two independent levers. (1) Stop making the theme a store subscription: read darkMode once in app/_layout.tsx and put the resolved Theme object in a React context, so useTheme becomes useContext with zero store subscribers — the theme object is already a stable module-level constant (tokens.ts darkTheme/lightTheme), so this is a drop-in change. (2) Virtualize the history list so only the visible cards are mounted.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** found by verifier, not original auditor

</details>

#### P3 (7)

<details><summary><b>Rest timer's 250 ms interval keeps firing after the countdown finishes</b> — <code>src/components/RestTimer.tsx:52</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `useEffect(() => { if (triggerKey === 0) return; const id = setInterval(() => { const left = Math.max(0, (deadlineRef.current - Date.now()) / 1000); setRemaining(left); ... }, 250); return () => clearInterval(id) }, [triggerKey])` — the only exit is `triggerKey === 0`, which is set solely by the user pressing X (`onDismiss={() => setRestKey(0)}` in app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:770). Once rest completes the interval keeps waking the JS thread four times a second forever. `ElapsedTimer` (workout.tsx:621-630) runs `setInterval(() => setNow(Date.now()), 1000)` with `[]` deps for as long as a session is open. Neither is gated on screen focus, and bottom-tab screens stay mounted.

**User impact.** After finishing one set, the phone's JS thread wakes 4×/second and re-renders a component indefinitely — including while the user is reading the Diary or Progress tab, and while the phone is in the user's pocket mid-workout. It is a steady background battery drain during exactly the hour the user needs the battery.

**Fix.** In RestTimer.tsx:52-63, clearInterval once `left <= 0` (firedRef at 41 already marks that moment) and let the [triggerKey] effect restart it on the next completed set. Not worth adding focus gating for.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** The leak is real: RestTimer.tsx:52-63 only bails on `triggerKey === 0`, and triggerKey is only reset by the user pressing X (workout.tsx:770 `onDismiss={() => setRestKey(0)}`), so the 250 ms interval keeps firing forever after the countdown completes. But the stated impact is wrong: once the deadline passes, `Math.max(0, ...)` pins `left` at exactly 0, so `setRemaining(0)` sets the same value and React bails out of re-rendering — it is a bare Date.now() wakeup 4x/second, not a component re-rendering indefinitely. That is a negligible battery cost, not 'a steady background drain during exactly the hour the user needs the battery'. ElapsedTimer (workout.tsx:621-630) is 1 Hz, bounded to an open session, and its own comment (616-620) explains it is deliberately isolated so it does not re-render the inputs. Demote to P3.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Progress re-derives its summaries every render and allocates element trees for the three unselected tabs</b> — <code>app/(tabs)/progress.tsx:756</code> · performance · effort M</summary>

**Code.** `const totalVolumeKg = sessionsInRange.reduce((total, s) => total + sessionVolume(s), 0)` and `const totalSets = sessionsInRange.reduce((total, s) => total + sessionSetCount(s), 0)` (756-757) sit in the render body with no `useMemo`, as does `const average = (pick) => ...` called six times (725-733), each a full pass over `dayStats`. `caloriesTab` (782), `macrosTab` (864), `weightTab` (979) and `trainingTab` (1143) are all constructed as JSX on every render even though only one is rendered (`{tab === 'calories' ? caloriesTab : null}` etc., 1261-1264) — and eager `.map`s inside them run regardless: `macroRows` (857), `recentPRs.map(pr => <View ...>)` (1194).

**User impact.** Toggling from the Calories tab to Macros — a pure UI switch — re-walks the whole 30-day diary summary six times and re-materialises the element trees for all four tabs. The segmented control feels heavier than it should, and every unrelated store write pays the same bill.

**Fix.** Wrap totalVolumeKg/totalSets and the six averages in one useMemo over [dayStats, sessionsInRange]. Converting the four tab bodies to components is optional cleanup — the heavy work is already correctly deferred inside ChartArea's render prop.

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Half right. The un-memoized derivations are real: progress.tsx:756-757 totalVolumeKg/totalSets reduce over sessionsInRange in the render body, and `average` (725-726) is called six times at 728-733, each a full pass over dayStats. The 'eager .map' claim is mostly FALSE: the expensive `dayStats.map(...)` at 810 and 891 sit inside ChartArea's render-prop callback `{width => (...)}` (805, 886) and are therefore never invoked for an unrendered tab — ChartArea (156-170) only calls children(width) when it is actually mounted. Only `macroRows` (857, 3 objects) and `recentPRs.map` (1194, <=4 rows) run eagerly, and dayStats is capped at 30 entries. So the total waste on a tab toggle is element allocation plus a few hundred arithmetic ops — real but not something a user perceives. P3.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The chat screen re-renders its whole transcript whenever any diary entry changes</b> — <code>app/chat.tsx:143</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `const goals = useStore(s => s.goals)`, `const diary = useStore(s => s.diary)`, `const profile = useStore(s => s.profile)`, `const currentWeightKg = useStore(s => s.currentWeightKg)` (143-146). None of the four is read during render — they are used only inside `send()` (`const day = diary[today]` at 182, `goals.calories` at 197, `profile.weightUnit` at 193/204/266). The transcript is rendered with `messages.map(...)` inside a `ScrollView` (327-487); each assistant turn can carry a `Surface` of logged actions with three `MacroChip`s.

**User impact.** When the assistant logs three foods, the store writes three times, and each write replaces the `diary` reference and re-renders the entire message list — including all previous bubbles and their macro chips — before the new bubble is even added. The 'Thinking…' → answer transition stutters on a long conversation.

**Fix.** Budget: assistant reply appears < 100ms after the network response resolves. Read these four values imperatively where they are used — `const { goals, diary, profile, currentWeightKg } = useStore.getState()` at the top of `send()` — and drop the four subscriptions. (The action selectors at 147-151 are stable and can stay.)

**Verifier (ADJUSTED).** Factually correct — I grepped chat.tsx and goals/diary/profile/currentWeightKg (143-146) are referenced only at 182, 193, 197-205 and 266-276, all inside `send()`, never during render. So the four subscriptions do force a full transcript re-render each time the assistant logs a food. But the impact is trivial: `messages` is a handful of bubbles in local state (155-157), and the three extra re-renders happen after a network round-trip that already took hundreds of ms. Nothing a user perceives. P3, and the proposed fix (read via useStore.getState() inside send) is correct and free.

</details>

<details><summary><b>Lift search re-ranks the full catalog on every keystroke and rebuilds renderItem</b> — <code>app/lift-picker.tsx:166</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `const results = useMemo(() => { ... const presets = searchLifts(query, LIFT_DATABASE.length) ... }, [query, muscle, equipment, customLifts])` (166-183) keys off raw `query`, not a debounced value — unlike food-search.tsx:266-269 which debounces at 350ms. `searchLifts` ranks and sorts all 128 entries of `LIFT_DATABASE` (src/core/data/exerciseDatabase.ts:162-177), then the result is filtered again by muscle and equipment. `renderItem={({ item }) => <LiftRow lift={item} onPress={() => handleSelect(item)} />}` (465) is an inline arrow, and `LiftRow` (70) is not memoized.

**User impact.** Each character typed re-ranks, re-sorts and re-filters 128 lifts and re-renders every visible row of the FlatList. At 128 items this is only a few milliseconds, but it is measurable on low-end hardware while the keyboard animation is also running, and the row re-renders are pure waste.

**Fix.** Budget: lift-search keystroke < 16ms. Reuse the debounce pattern from food-search (`useEffect` + 200ms `setTimeout` into a `debouncedQuery`), memoize `renderItem` with `useCallback`, and wrap `LiftRow` in `React.memo` with a stable `onPress={handleSelect}` that the row invokes with its own `lift`.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. lift-picker.tsx:166-183 keys `results` off raw `query` (135), unlike food-search.tsx:266-269 which debounces at 350 ms. searchLifts (exerciseDatabase.ts:162-177) ranks every LIFT_DATABASE entry then sorts, and the result is filtered again by muscle/equipment at 178-182. renderItem is an inline arrow at lift-picker.tsx:465 and LiftRow (70) is not memoized. The finding correctly calls this small in absolute terms and rates it P3.

</details>

<details><summary><b>formatDate goes through Intl and is called once per list row</b> — <code>src/core/utils/calculations.ts:137</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `export function formatDate(dateStr: string): string { const [year, month, day] = dateStr.split('-').map(Number); const date = new Date(year, month - 1, day); return date.toLocaleDateString('en-US', { month: 'short', day: 'numeric' }) }` — a `Date` allocation plus a Hermes `Intl.DateTimeFormat` resolution per call, with no format cache. Callers per render: app/(tabs)/workout.tsx:916 and 934 (once per HistoryCard, ×78 sessions), 1265 (per PR row), app/(tabs)/profile.tsx:554 and 627 (per weight entry and per measurement, up to 25), app/(tabs)/progress.tsx:118/121 (axis) and 1209 (per PR). Note the app deliberately avoids Intl for numbers for exactly this class of reason (see the `formatNumber` comment at app/(tabs)/index.tsx:36-42).

**User impact.** On the Workout tab with 78 history cards that is 156 Intl formatter resolutions per render of the list — small individually, but it compounds with the per-keystroke re-render cascade above and is pure repeated work for a fixed set of dates.

**Fix.** Budget: date formatting < 0.5ms per list render. This file is generated (src/core/**), so raise it as an upstream web-repo change: hoist a single module-level `const FORMATTER = new Intl.DateTimeFormat('en-US', { month: 'short', day: 'numeric' })` and reuse it, or format from the parsed parts against a static month-name table the way `getTodayString` already builds strings by hand.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** Verified. calculations.ts:137-141 is exactly as quoted: split/map/Number, a new Date, then date.toLocaleDateString('en-US', {...}) with no cached formatter. Callers verified at workout.tsx:916, 934 and 1265; profile.tsx:554, 559, 627, 636; progress.tsx:117, 120 and 1209. index.tsx:34-42 does document the deliberate avoidance of Intl for numbers, so the inconsistency is real. Correctly raised as an upstream src/core change rather than an editable defect. P3 is the right weight.

</details>

<details><summary><b>The rest countdown re-renders four times a second to display a value that changes once a second</b> — <code>src/components/RestTimer.tsx:54</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `const id = setInterval(() => { const left = Math.max(0, (deadlineRef.current - Date.now()) / 1000); setRemaining(left); ... }, 250)` (54-61) stores `left` as a float, so the state value differs on every tick and React re-renders. The only consumers are `format(remaining)` (98-100), which rounds to whole seconds, and ProgressTrack (109-113), a 5 px bar. Three of every four renders produce a pixel-identical frame.

**User impact.** During every rest period between sets the JS thread does four renders per second, plus the parent's re-render of the surrounding View, for a clock that visibly ticks once per second — wasted work at exactly the moment the user may also be typing the previous set's numbers.

**Fix.** Either drop the interval to 1000 ms (the wall-clock deadline design at line 55 already makes drift a non-issue, which is the whole point of the comment at 28-35), or keep the 250 ms poll and store `Math.ceil(left)` so setRemaining bails out on the three redundant ticks. Fold this in with the never-stops fix in the same effect.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** found by verifier, not original auditor

</details>

<details><summary><b>useHealthSync subscribes to profile and weightLog for a function neither StepsCard nor the dashboard ever calls</b> — <code>src/hooks/useHealthSync.ts:35</code> · performance · effort S</summary>

**Code.** `const profile = useStore(s => s.profile)` and `const weightLog = useStore(s => s.weightLog)` (35-36) exist only to feed importWeightHistory's useCallback deps (87-100). StepsCard (src/components/StepsCard.tsx:22-26) destructures availability/granted/todaySteps/weekSteps/busy/connect/refreshSteps and never touches importWeightHistory, yet it inherits both subscriptions on top of its own `useStore(s => s.profile)` at line 24.

**User impact.** Every weigh-in the user logs — including each of the N writes in a Health Connect import — re-renders the dashboard's StepsCard and re-creates its three useCallbacks, for data it does not display.

**Fix.** Read the two values imperatively inside importWeightHistory via useStore.getState() and drop the subscriptions (the callback then has [addWeightEntry] as its only dep). Pairs naturally with hoisting the hook into a shared singleton.

**Verifier (CONFIRMED).** found by verifier, not original auditor

</details>
